      If \(a * b = b\), then \(a = e\), which is a contradiction.
      Therefore, \(a * b = c\).
      Similarly, \(b * a = c\), \(a * c = b\), \(c * a = b\), \(b * c = a\), and \(c * b = a\).
      This determines the group structure, which is isomorphic to \((\setZ_{2} \times \setZ_{2}, +)\).
    \end{itemize}
    Therefore, there are exactly two groups of order \(4\) up to isomorphism.
    
    \textbf{Order 5:} There is exactly one group of order \(5\) up to isomorphism, namely \((\setZ_{5}, +)\).
    \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{|c|c|c|c|c|c|}
    \hline
    \(+\) & \([0]\) & \([1]\) & \([2]\) & \([3]\) & \([4]\) \\
    \hline
    \([0]\) & \([0]\) & \([1]\) & \([2]\) & \([3]\) & \([4]\) \\
    \hline
    \([1]\) & \([1]\) & \([2]\) & \([3]\) & \([4]\) & \([0]\) \\
    \hline
    \([2]\) & \([2]\) & \([3]\) & \([4]\) & \([0]\) & \([1]\) \\
    \hline
    \([3]\) & \([3]\) & \([4]\) & \([0]\) & \([1]\) & \([2]\) \\
    \hline
    \([4]\) & \([4]\) & \([0]\) & \([1]\) & \([2]\) & \([3]\) \\
    \hline
    \end{tabular}
    \end{center}
    Let \(G\) be a group of order \(5\).
    Since \(|G| = 5\) is prime, \(G\) must be \nameref{def:cyclic-group} generated by any non-identity element.
    Therefore, \(G\) is isomorphic to \((\setZ_{5}, +)\).
    
    \textbf{Summary:}
    \begin{itemize}
      \item Order \(1\): \(1\) group (trivial group)
      \item Order \(2\): \(1\) group \((\setZ_{2}, +)\)
      \item Order \(3\): \(1\) group \((\setZ_{3}, +)\)
      \item Order \(4\): \(2\) groups \((\setZ_{4}, +)\) and \((\setZ_{2} \times \setZ_{2}, +)\)
      \item Order \(5\): \(1\) group \((\setZ_{5}, +)\)
    \end{itemize}
  \end{subprove}
  \item Consider the dihedral group of order \(8\): \(D_{8} = \langle a, x \mid a^{2} = x^{4} = e, a x a^{-1} = x^{-1} \rangle\) with identity \(e\), generators \(a\) and \(x\), and relations \(a^{2} = e, x^{4} = e\), and \(a x a^{-1} = x^{-1}\).
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item List all 10 \nameref{def:subgroup}s of \(D_{8}\).
    \begin{subprove}
      All 10 \nameref{def:subgroup}s of \(D_{8}\) will be listed.
      
      First, the elements of \(D_{8}\) will be determined.
      From the relations \(a^{2} = e\) and \(x^{4} = e\), and the relation \(a x a^{-1} = x^{-1}\), it follows that \(a x = x^{-1} a\).
      Since \(a^{2} = e\), it follows that \(a = a^{-1}\).
      The elements of \(D_{8}\) are:
      \begin{itemize}
        \item \(e\) (identity)
        \item \(x, x^{2}, x^{3}\) (powers of \(x\), rotations)
        \item \(a, a x, a x^{2}, a x^{3}\) (reflections)
      \end{itemize}
      Therefore, \(|D_{8}| = 8\).
      
      Now, all \nameref{def:subgroup}s of \(D_{8}\) will be listed:
      \begin{enumerate}
        \item \(\{e\}\) — the trivial subgroup (order 1).
        \item \(\{e, x^{2}\}\) — the \nameref{def:cyclic-subgroup} generated by \(x^{2}\), which has order 2 (order 2).
        \item \(\{e, x, x^{2}, x^{3}\}\) — the \nameref{def:cyclic-subgroup} generated by \(x\), which is the subgroup of rotations (order 4).
        \item \(\{e, a\}\) — the \nameref{def:cyclic-subgroup} generated by \(a\), which has order 2 (order 2).
        \item \(\{e, a x\}\) — the \nameref{def:cyclic-subgroup} generated by \(a x\), which has order 2 (order 2).
        \item \(\{e, a x^{2}\}\) — the \nameref{def:cyclic-subgroup} generated by \(a x^{2}\), which has order 2 (order 2).
        \item \(\{e, a x^{3}\}\) — the \nameref{def:cyclic-subgroup} generated by \(a x^{3}\), which has order 2 (order 2).
        \item \(\{e, x^{2}, a, a x^{2}\}\) — a subgroup of order 4 (order 4).
        \item \(\{e, x^{2}, a x, a x^{3}\}\) — a subgroup of order 4 (order 4).
        \item \(D_{8}\) — the group itself (order 8).
      \end{enumerate}
      
      It will be verified that items 8 and 9 are indeed subgroups.
      For item 8: \(\{e, x^{2}, a, a x^{2}\}\).
      \begin{itemize}
        \item \(e \in H\), so \(H \neq \emptyset\).
        \item Closure: \(x^{2} \cdot x^{2} = x^{4} = e\), \(a \cdot a = a^{2} = e\), \((a x^{2}) \cdot (a x^{2}) = a x^{2} a x^{2} = a x^{2} a x^{2} = a (x^{2} a) x^{2} = a (a x^{-2}) x^{2} = a^{2} x^{-2} x^{2} = e\).
        \item \(x^{2} \cdot a = x^{2} a = a x^{-2} = a x^{2}\) (since \(x^{-2} = x^{2}\)).
        \item \(a \cdot x^{2} = a x^{2}\).
        \item \(x^{2} \cdot (a x^{2}) = x^{2} a x^{2} = a x^{-2} x^{2} = a\).
        \item \((a x^{2}) \cdot x^{2} = a x^{2} x^{2} = a x^{4} = a\).
        \item \(a \cdot (a x^{2}) = a^{2} x^{2} = x^{2}\).
        \item \((a x^{2}) \cdot a = a x^{2} a = a x^{2} a = a (x^{2} a) = a (a x^{-2}) = a^{2} x^{-2} = x^{2}\).
      \end{itemize}
      Therefore, \(\{e, x^{2}, a, a x^{2}\}\) is closed under multiplication and contains inverses, so it is a subgroup.
      
      For item 9: \(\{e, x^{2}, a x, a x^{3}\}\).
      \begin{itemize}
        \item \(e \in H\), so \(H \neq \emptyset\).
        \item Closure: \(x^{2} \cdot x^{2} = e\), \((a x) \cdot (a x) = a x a x = a (x a) x = a (a x^{-1}) x = a^{2} x^{-1} x = e\), \((a x^{3}) \cdot (a x^{3}) = a x^{3} a x^{3} = a (x^{3} a) x^{3} = a (a x^{-3}) x^{3} = a^{2} x^{-3} x^{3} = e\).
        \item \(x^{2} \cdot (a x) = x^{2} a x = a x^{-2} x = a x^{-1} = a x^{3}\).
        \item \((a x) \cdot x^{2} = a x^{3}\).
        \item \(x^{2} \cdot (a x^{3}) = x^{2} a x^{3} = a x^{-2} x^{3} = a x = a x\).
        \item \((a x^{3}) \cdot x^{2} = a x^{5} = a x\).
        \item \((a x) \cdot (a x^{3}) = a x a x^{3} = a (x a) x^{3} = a (a x^{-1}) x^{3} = a^{2} x^{2} = x^{2}\).
        \item \((a x^{3}) \cdot (a x) = a x^{3} a x = a (x^{3} a) x = a (a x^{-3}) x = a^{2} x^{-2} = x^{2}\).
      \end{itemize}
      Therefore, \(\{e, x^{2}, a x, a x^{3}\}\) is closed under multiplication and contains inverses, so it is a subgroup.
      
      These are all the \nameref{def:subgroup}s of \(D_{8}\).
    \end{subprove}
    \item Which \nameref{def:subgroup}s of \(D_{8}\) are isomorphic to the Klein 4-group? Justify your answer.
    \begin{subprove}
      It will be determined which \nameref{def:subgroup}s of \(D_{8}\) are isomorphic to the Klein 4-group.
      
      The Klein 4-group is \((\setZ_{2} \times \setZ_{2}, +)\), which has the following properties:
      \begin{itemize}
        \item It has order 4.
        \item It is abelian.
        \item Every non-identity element has order 2.
      \end{itemize}
      
      The \nameref{def:subgroup}s of \(D_{8}\) of order 4 are:
      \begin{itemize}
        \item \(\{e, x, x^{2}, x^{3}\}\) — This is cyclic (generated by \(x\)), so it is isomorphic to \((\setZ_{4}, +)\), not to the Klein 4-group.
        \item \(\{e, x^{2}, a, a x^{2}\}\) — This has order 4, and all non-identity elements have order 2 (since \(x^{2} \cdot x^{2} = e\), \(a \cdot a = e\), and \((a x^{2}) \cdot (a x^{2}) = e\)).
        \item \(\{e, x^{2}, a x, a x^{3}\}\) — This has order 4, and all non-identity elements have order 2 (since \(x^{2} \cdot x^{2} = e\), \((a x) \cdot (a x) = e\), and \((a x^{3}) \cdot (a x^{3}) = e\)).
      \end{itemize}
      
      It will be verified that \(\{e, x^{2}, a, a x^{2}\}\) is isomorphic to the Klein 4-group.
      Since \(|H| = 4\) and all non-identity elements have order 2, and since \(H\) is abelian (as verified by checking that \(x^{2} \cdot a = a \cdot x^{2} = a x^{2}\)), it follows that \(H\) is isomorphic to \((\setZ_{2} \times \setZ_{2}, +)\).
      
      Similarly, \(\{e, x^{2}, a x, a x^{3}\}\) is isomorphic to the Klein 4-group.
      Since \(|H| = 4\) and all non-identity elements have order 2, and since \(H\) is abelian (as verified by checking that \(x^{2} \cdot (a x) = (a x) \cdot x^{2} = a x^{3}\)), it follows that \(H\) is isomorphic to \((\setZ_{2} \times \setZ_{2}, +)\).
      
      Therefore, the \nameref{def:subgroup}s of \(D_{8}\) that are isomorphic to the Klein 4-group are:
      \begin{itemize}
        \item \(\{e, x^{2}, a, a x^{2}\}\)
        \item \(\{e, x^{2}, a x, a x^{3}\}\)
      \end{itemize}
    \end{subprove}
  \end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}





\newpage
\begin{define}{Mathematical Induction}[def:mathematical-induction][red]
  \textbf{Mathematical induction} is a method of proof used to prove statements about natural numbers.
  To prove that a statement \(P(n)\) is true for all natural numbers \(n \geq n_{0}\), where \(n_{0}\) is some base value, it suffices to show that
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Base case:} \(P(n_{0})\) is true, and
    \item \textbf{Inductive step:} For any \(k \geq n_{0}\), if \(P(k)\) is true, then \(P(k+1)\) is true.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{define}

\begin{define}{Strong Mathematical Induction}[def:strong-induction][red]
  \textbf{Strong mathematical induction} (also called \textbf{complete induction}) is a method of proof used to prove statements about natural numbers.
  To prove that a statement \(P(n)\) is true for all natural numbers \(n \geq n_{0}\), where \(n_{0}\) is some base value, it suffices to show that
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Base case:} \(P(n_{0})\) is true, and
    \item \textbf{Inductive step:} For any \(k \geq n_{0}\), if \(P(m)\) is true for all \(m\) such that \(n_{0} \leq m \leq k\), then \(P(k+1)\) is true.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{define}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item Find a formula for the following summations.
  Prove your formula is correct using \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}.
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item \(1 + 2 + 3 + \ldots + n\) for all integers \(n \geq 1\).
    \begin{subprove}
      It will be proved that \(1 + 2 + 3 + \ldots + n = \frac{n(n+1)}{2}\) for all integers \(n \geq 1\).
      
      Let \(P(n)\) be the proposition that \(1 + 2 + 3 + \ldots + n = \frac{n(n+1)}{2}\).
      
      \textbf{Base case:} For \(n = 1\), the left-hand side is \(1\), and the right-hand side is \(\frac{1(1+1)}{2} = \frac{2}{2} = 1\).
      Therefore, \(P(1)\) is true.
      
      \textbf{Inductive step:} Suppose that \(P(k)\) is true for some integer \(k \geq 1\).
      That is, suppose that \(1 + 2 + 3 + \ldots + k = \frac{k(k+1)}{2}\).
      It will be proved that \(P(k+1)\) is true.
      That is, it will be proved that \(1 + 2 + 3 + \ldots + (k+1) = \frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}\).
      
      By the inductive hypothesis, \(1 + 2 + 3 + \ldots + k = \frac{k(k+1)}{2}\).
      Therefore, \(1 + 2 + 3 + \ldots + (k+1) = (1 + 2 + 3 + \ldots + k) + (k+1) = \frac{k(k+1)}{2} + (k+1) = \frac{k(k+1) + 2(k+1)}{2} = \frac{(k+1)(k+2)}{2}\).
      
      Therefore, \(P(k+1)\) is true.
      
      By \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}, it follows that \(P(n)\) is true for all integers \(n \geq 1\).
    \end{subprove}
    \item \(1 + 2 + 2^{2} + \ldots + 2^{n}\) for all nonnegative integers \(n\).
    \begin{subprove}
      It will be proved that \(1 + 2 + 2^{2} + \ldots + 2^{n} = 2^{n+1} - 1\) for all nonnegative integers \(n\).
      
      Let \(P(n)\) be the proposition that \(1 + 2 + 2^{2} + \ldots + 2^{n} = 2^{n+1} - 1\).
      
      \textbf{Base case:} For \(n = 0\), the left-hand side is \(1\), and the right-hand side is \(2^{0+1} - 1 = 2 - 1 = 1\).
      Therefore, \(P(0)\) is true.
      
      \textbf{Inductive step:} Suppose that \(P(k)\) is true for some nonnegative integer \(k\).
      That is, suppose that \(1 + 2 + 2^{2} + \ldots + 2^{k} = 2^{k+1} - 1\).
      It will be proved that \(P(k+1)\) is true.
      That is, it will be proved that \(1 + 2 + 2^{2} + \ldots + 2^{k+1} = 2^{k+2} - 1\).
      
      By the inductive hypothesis, \(1 + 2 + 2^{2} + \ldots + 2^{k} = 2^{k+1} - 1\).
      Therefore, \(1 + 2 + 2^{2} + \ldots + 2^{k+1} = (1 + 2 + 2^{2} + \ldots + 2^{k}) + 2^{k+1} = (2^{k+1} - 1) + 2^{k+1} = 2 \cdot 2^{k+1} - 1 = 2^{k+2} - 1\).
      
      Therefore, \(P(k+1)\) is true.
      
      By \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}, it follows that \(P(n)\) is true for all nonnegative integers \(n\).
    \end{subprove}
  \end{enumerate}
  \item For which nonnegative integers \(n\) do the following inequalities hold?
  Prove your answer is correct using \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}.
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item \(2 n \leq n^{2}\)
    \begin{subprove}
      It will be determined for which nonnegative integers \(n\) the inequality \(2 n \leq n^{2}\) holds, and it will be proved using \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}.
      Let \(P(n)\) be the proposition that \(2 n \leq n^{2}\).
      
      \textbf{Base case:} It will be proved that \(P(2)\) is true.
      \(2 \cdot 2 = 4\) and \(2^{2} = 4\), so \(2 \cdot 2 = 4 \leq 4 = 2^{2}\).
      Therefore, \(P(2)\) is true.
      
      \textbf{Inductive step:} It will be proved that for any integer \(k \geq 2\), if \(P(k)\) is true, then \(P(k+1)\) is true.
      Suppose that \(P(k)\) is true for some integer \(k \geq 2\), that is, \(2 k \leq k^{2}\).
      It will be proved that \(P(k+1)\) is true, that is, \(2(k+1) \leq (k+1)^{2}\).
      
      Since \(2 k \leq k^{2}\), it follows that \(2 k + 2 \leq k^{2} + 2\).
      Since \(k \geq 2\), it follows that \(2 \leq 2k\), so \(k^{2} + 2 \leq k^{2} + 2k\).
      Since \(k \geq 2\), it follows that \(2k \geq 4\), so \(k^{2} + 2k \leq k^{2} + 2k + 1 = (k+1)^{2}\).
      Therefore, \(2 k + 2 = 2(k+1) \leq (k+1)^{2}\).
      Therefore, \(P(k+1)\) is true.
      
      By \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}, it follows that \(P(n)\) is true for all integers \(n \geq 2\).
      
      Therefore, the inequality \(2 n \leq n^{2}\) holds for all nonnegative integers \(n\) with \(n = 0\) or \(n \geq 2\).
    \end{subprove}
    \item \(n^{2} < 2^{n}\)
    \begin{subprove}
      It will be determined for which nonnegative integers \(n\) the inequality \(n^{2} < 2^{n}\) holds, and it will be proved using \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}.
      Let \(P(n)\) be the proposition that \(n^{2} < 2^{n}\).
      
      \textbf{Base case:} It will be proved that \(P(5)\) is true.
      \(5^{2} = 25\) and \(2^{5} = 32\), so \(25 < 32\).
      Therefore, \(P(5)\) is true.
      
      \textbf{Inductive step:} It will be proved that for any integer \(k \geq 5\), if \(P(k)\) is true, then \(P(k+1)\) is true.
      Suppose that \(P(k)\) is true for some integer \(k \geq 5\), that is, \(k^{2} < 2^{k}\).
      It will be proved that \(P(k+1)\) is true, that is, \((k+1)^{2} < 2^{k+1}\).
      
      Since \(k^{2} < 2^{k}\), it follows that \(2 k^{2} < 2^{k+1}\).
      It will be proved that \((k+1)^{2} < 2 k^{2}\).
      Since \((k+1)^{2} = k^{2} + 2k + 1\), it suffices to show that \(k^{2} + 2k + 1 < 2 k^{2}\), that is, \(2k + 1 < k^{2}\), that is, \(k^{2} - 2k - 1 > 0\).
      Since \(k \geq 5\), it follows that \(k^{2} - 2k - 1 = k(k-2) - 1 \geq 5(5-2) - 1 = 15 - 1 = 14 > 0\).
      Therefore, \((k+1)^{2} < 2 k^{2} < 2^{k+1}\).
      Therefore, \(P(k+1)\) is true.
      
      By \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}, it follows that \(P(n)\) is true for all integers \(n \geq 5\).
      
      Therefore, the inequality \(n^{2} < 2^{n}\) holds for all nonnegative integers \(n\) with \(n = 0, 1\), or \(n \geq 5\).
    \end{subprove}
    \item \(2^{n} \leq n!\)
    \begin{subprove}
      It will be determined for which nonnegative integers \(n\) the inequality \(2^{n} \leq n!\) holds, and it will be proved using \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}.
      Let \(P(n)\) be the proposition that \(2^{n} \leq n!\).
      
      \textbf{Base case:} It will be proved that \(P(4)\) is true.
      \(2^{4} = 16\) and \(4! = 24\), so \(16 \leq 24\).
      Therefore, \(P(4)\) is true.
      
      \textbf{Inductive step:} It will be proved that for any integer \(k \geq 4\), if \(P(k)\) is true, then \(P(k+1)\) is true.
      Suppose that \(P(k)\) is true for some integer \(k \geq 4\), that is, \(2^{k} \leq k!\).
      It will be proved that \(P(k+1)\) is true, that is, \(2^{k+1} \leq (k+1)!\).
      
      Since \(2^{k} \leq k!\), it follows that \(2 \cdot 2^{k} = 2^{k+1} \leq 2 \cdot k!\).
      Since \(k \geq 4\), it follows that \(k + 1 \geq 5 > 2\), so \(2 \cdot k! \leq (k+1) \cdot k! = (k+1)!\).
      Therefore, \(2^{k+1} \leq (k+1)!\).
      Therefore, \(P(k+1)\) is true.
      
      By \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}, it follows that \(P(n)\) is true for all integers \(n \geq 4\).
      
      Therefore, the inequality \(2^{n} \leq n!\) holds for all nonnegative integers \(n\) with \(n = 0\) or \(n \geq 4\).
    \end{subprove}
    \item \(n! < n^{n}\)
    \begin{subprove}
      It will be determined for which nonnegative integers \(n\) the inequality \(n! < n^{n}\) holds, and it will be proved using \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}.
      
      First, small values will be checked:
      \begin{itemize}
        \item For \(n = 0\): \(0! = 1\) and \(0^{0}\) is undefined, so the inequality does not apply.
        \item For \(n = 1\): \(1! = 1\) is not less than \(1 = 1^{1}\), so the inequality does not hold.
        \item For \(n = 2\): \(2! = 2 < 4 = 2^{2}\), so the inequality holds.
        \item For \(n = 3\): \(3! = 6 < 27 = 3^{3}\), so the inequality holds.
      \end{itemize}
      
      It will be conjectured that \(n! < n^{n}\) holds for all integers \(n \geq 2\).
      It will be proved using \nameref{def:mathematical-induction} that \(n! < n^{n}\) holds for all integers \(n \geq 2\).
      
      Let \(P(n)\) be the proposition that \(n! < n^{n}\).
      
      \textbf{Base case:} It will be proved that \(P(2)\) is true.
      \(2! = 2\) and \(2^{2} = 4\), so \(2 < 4\).
      Therefore, \(P(2)\) is true.
      
      \textbf{Inductive step:} It will be proved that for any integer \(k \geq 2\), if \(P(k)\) is true, then \(P(k+1)\) is true.
      Suppose that \(P(k)\) is true for some integer \(k \geq 2\), that is, \(k! < k^{k}\).
      It will be proved that \(P(k+1)\) is true, that is, \((k+1)! < (k+1)^{k+1}\).
      
      Since \(k! < k^{k}\), it follows that \((k+1) \cdot k! = (k+1)! < (k+1) \cdot k^{k}\).
      It will be proved that \((k+1) \cdot k^{k} < (k+1)^{k+1}\).
      Since \((k+1)^{k+1} = (k+1) \cdot (k+1)^{k}\), it suffices to show that \(k^{k} < (k+1)^{k}\), that is, \(\left(\frac{k}{k+1}\right)^{k} < 1\).
      Since \(k \geq 2\), it follows that \(\frac{k}{k+1} < 1\), so \(\left(\frac{k}{k+1}\right)^{k} < 1\).
      Therefore, \((k+1)! < (k+1) \cdot k^{k} < (k+1) \cdot (k+1)^{k} = (k+1)^{k+1}\).
      Therefore, \(P(k+1)\) is true.
      
      By \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}, it follows that \(P(n)\) is true for all integers \(n \geq 2\).
      
      Therefore, the inequality \(n! < n^{n}\) holds for all integers \(n \geq 2\).
    \end{subprove}
  \end{enumerate}
  \item Consider the following claim and proof.
  \textbf{Claim:} All horses are the same color.
  \textbf{Proof:} Let \(P(n)\) be the proposition that all horses in a set of \(n\) horses are the same color.
  Clearly, \(P(1)\) is true.
  Now assume that \(P(n)\) is true, so that all horses in any set of \(n\) horses are the same color.
  Consider any \(n+1\) horses; number these as horses \(1, 2, 3, \ldots, n, n+1\).
  Now the first \(n\) of these horses all must have the same color, and the last \(n\) of these must also have the same color.
  Since the set of the first \(n\) horses and the last \(n\) horses overlap, all \(n+1\) must be the same color.
  This shows that \(P(n+1)\) is true and finishes the proof by induction.\\  
  Is this a proof or a spoof? Justify your answer.
  \begin{subprove}
    It will be determined whether this is a proof or a spoof, and the error will be identified.
    
    This is a spoof (a false proof).
    The error lies in the inductive step when attempting to prove \(P(2)\) from \(P(1)\).
    
    The inductive step claims that for any \(n+1\) horses, the first \(n\) horses all have the same color, and the last \(n\) horses all have the same color.
    It then claims that since these two sets overlap, all \(n+1\) horses must have the same color.
    
    However, when \(n = 1\) and \(n+1 = 2\), the argument fails:
    \begin{itemize}
      \item The first \(n = 1\) horse is horse \(1\).
      \item The last \(n = 1\) horse is horse \(2\).
      \item The set \(\{horse 1\}\) and the set \(\{horse 2\}\) do not overlap.
      \item Therefore, the argument that "since the sets overlap, all \(n+1\) must be the same color" cannot be applied.
    \end{itemize}
    
    The inductive step requires that the sets of the first \(n\) horses and the last \(n\) horses overlap, which means they must share at least one horse in common.
    For \(n+1\) horses, the first \(n\) horses are horses \(1, 2, \ldots, n\), and the last \(n\) horses are horses \(2, 3, \ldots, n+1\).
    These sets overlap if and only if they share at least one horse, which occurs when \(n \geq 2\).
    However, to prove \(P(2)\) from \(P(1)\) using the inductive step, we need \(n = 1\), and in this case, the sets \(\{horse 1\}\) and \(\{horse 2\}\) do not overlap.
    
    Therefore, the inductive step fails when going from \(P(1)\) to \(P(2)\), and the proof is invalid.
    This is a spoof, not a valid proof.
  \end{subprove}
  \item Prove that \(n^{2} - 1\) is divisible by \(8\) whenever \(n\) is an odd positive integer.
  \begin{subprove}
    It will be proved that \(n^{2} - 1\) is divisible by \(8\) for all odd positive integers \(n\).
    
    Let \(n\) be an odd positive integer.
    Then there exists an integer \(k \geq 0\) such that \(n = 2k + 1\).
    Therefore, \(n^{2} - 1 = (2k + 1)^{2} - 1 = 4k^{2} + 4k + 1 - 1 = 4k(k + 1)\).
    
    It will be proved that \(4k(k + 1)\) is divisible by \(8\).
    Since \(k\) and \(k + 1\) are consecutive integers, one of them is even.
    Therefore, \(k(k + 1)\) is even, so there exists an integer \(m\) such that \(k(k + 1) = 2m\).
    Therefore, \(4k(k + 1) = 4 \cdot 2m = 8m\), which is divisible by \(8\).
    
    Therefore, \(n^{2} - 1\) is divisible by \(8\).
  \end{subprove}
  \item Consider the problem of determining which amounts of postage can be formed using just \(5\)-cent and \(6\)-cent stamps.
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item Determine which amounts of postage can be formed using just \(5\)-cent and \(6\)-cent stamps.
    \begin{subprove}
      It will be determined which amounts of postage can be formed using just \(5\)-cent and \(6\)-cent stamps.
      
      Small amounts will be checked systematically:
      \begin{itemize}
        \item \(5\): Yes, using one \(5\)-cent stamp.
        \item \(6\): Yes, using one \(6\)-cent stamp.
        \item \(7\): No, cannot be formed.
        \item \(8\): No, cannot be formed.
        \item \(9\): No, cannot be formed.
        \item \(10\): Yes, using two \(5\)-cent stamps.
        \item \(11\): Yes, using one \(5\)-cent stamp and one \(6\)-cent stamp.
        \item \(12\): Yes, using two \(6\)-cent stamps.
        \item \(13\): No, cannot be formed (would require \(5 + 8\) or \(6 + 7\), neither of which can be formed).
        \item \(14\): No, cannot be formed (would require \(5 + 9\) or \(6 + 8\), neither of which can be formed).
        \item \(15\): Yes, using three \(5\)-cent stamps.
        \item \(16\): Yes, using two \(5\)-cent stamps and one \(6\)-cent stamp.
        \item \(17\): Yes, using one \(5\)-cent stamp and two \(6\)-cent stamps.
        \item \(18\): Yes, using three \(6\)-cent stamps.
        \item \(19\): No, cannot be formed (would require \(5 + 14\), \(6 + 13\), \(10 + 9\), \(11 + 8\), \(12 + 7\), none of which can be formed).
        \item \(20\): Yes, using four \(5\)-cent stamps.
        \item \(21\): Yes, using three \(5\)-cent stamps and one \(6\)-cent stamp.
        \item \(22\): Yes, using two \(5\)-cent stamps and two \(6\)-cent stamps.
        \item \(23\): Yes, using one \(5\)-cent stamp and three \(6\)-cent stamps.
        \item \(24\): Yes, using four \(6\)-cent stamps.
      \end{itemize}
      
      It will be conjectured that all amounts of postage \(n \geq 20\) can be formed using just \(5\)-cent and \(6\)-cent stamps.
      The amounts that cannot be formed are: \(1, 2, 3, 4, 7, 8, 9, 13, 14, 19\).
      
      This conjecture will be proved in the next parts using \nameref{def:mathematical-induction} and \nameref{def:strong-induction}.
    \end{subprove}
    \item Prove your answer is correct using \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}.
    \begin{subprove}
      It will be proved using \nameref{def:mathematical-induction} that all amounts of postage \(n \geq 20\) can be formed using just \(5\)-cent and \(6\)-cent stamps.
      
      Let \(P(n)\) be the proposition that \(n\) cents of postage can be formed using just \(5\)-cent and \(6\)-cent stamps.
      It will be proved that \(P(n)\) is true for all integers \(n \geq 20\).
      
      \textbf{Base cases:} It will be proved that \(P(20)\), \(P(21)\), \(P(22)\), \(P(23)\), and \(P(24)\) are true.
      \begin{itemize}
        \item \(P(20)\): \(20 = 4 \cdot 5\), so \(20\) can be formed using four \(5\)-cent stamps.
        \item \(P(21)\): \(21 = 3 \cdot 5 + 1 \cdot 6\), so \(21\) can be formed using three \(5\)-cent stamps and one \(6\)-cent stamp.
        \item \(P(22)\): \(22 = 2 \cdot 5 + 2 \cdot 6\), so \(22\) can be formed using two \(5\)-cent stamps and two \(6\)-cent stamps.
        \item \(P(23)\): \(23 = 1 \cdot 5 + 3 \cdot 6\), so \(23\) can be formed using one \(5\)-cent stamp and three \(6\)-cent stamps.
        \item \(P(24)\): \(24 = 4 \cdot 6\), so \(24\) can be formed using four \(6\)-cent stamps.
      \end{itemize}
      Therefore, all base cases are true.
      
      \textbf{Inductive step:} It will be proved that for any integer \(k \geq 24\), if \(P(k)\) is true, then \(P(k+1)\) is true.
      Suppose that \(P(k)\) is true for some integer \(k \geq 24\), that is, \(k\) cents of postage can be formed using just \(5\)-cent and \(6\)-cent stamps.
      It will be proved that \(P(k+1)\) is true, that is, \(k+1\) cents of postage can be formed using just \(5\)-cent and \(6\)-cent stamps.
      
      Since \(k \geq 24\), consider any representation of \(k\) cents using \(5\)-cent and \(6\)-cent stamps.
      Two cases will be considered:
      \begin{itemize}
        \item \textbf{Case 1:} The representation uses at least one \(5\)-cent stamp.
        Then by replacing one \(5\)-cent stamp with one \(6\)-cent stamp, \(k+1 = k - 5 + 6\) cents can be formed.
        \item \textbf{Case 2:} The representation uses no \(5\)-cent stamps (only \(6\)-cent stamps).
        Since \(k \geq 24\) and \(k\) is formed using only \(6\)-cent stamps, there are at least \(\lceil 24/6 \rceil = 4\) stamps.
        By replacing four \(6\)-cent stamps (totaling \(24\) cents) with five \(5\)-cent stamps (totaling \(25\) cents), \(k+1 = k - 24 + 25 = k + 1\) cents can be formed.
      \end{itemize}
      In both cases, \(k+1\) cents can be formed.
      Therefore, \(P(k+1)\) is true.
      
      By \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}, it follows that \(P(n)\) is true for all integers \(n \geq 20\).
      
      Therefore, all amounts of postage \(n \geq 20\) can be formed using just \(5\)-cent and \(6\)-cent stamps.
    \end{subprove}
    \item Prove your answer is correct using \nameref{def:strong-induction}.
    \begin{subprove}
      It will be proved using \nameref{def:strong-induction} that all amounts of postage \(n \geq 20\) can be formed using just \(5\)-cent and \(6\)-cent stamps.
      
      Let \(P(n)\) be the proposition that \(n\) cents of postage can be formed using just \(5\)-cent and \(6\)-cent stamps.
      It will be proved that \(P(n)\) is true for all integers \(n \geq 20\).
      
      \textbf{Base cases:} It will be proved that \(P(20)\), \(P(21)\), \(P(22)\), \(P(23)\), and \(P(24)\) are true.
      \begin{itemize}
        \item \(P(20)\): \(20 = 4 \cdot 5\), so \(20\) can be formed using four \(5\)-cent stamps.
        \item \(P(21)\): \(21 = 3 \cdot 5 + 1 \cdot 6\), so \(21\) can be formed using three \(5\)-cent stamps and one \(6\)-cent stamp.
        \item \(P(22)\): \(22 = 2 \cdot 5 + 2 \cdot 6\), so \(22\) can be formed using two \(5\)-cent stamps and two \(6\)-cent stamps.
        \item \(P(23)\): \(23 = 1 \cdot 5 + 3 \cdot 6\), so \(23\) can be formed using one \(5\)-cent stamp and three \(6\)-cent stamps.
        \item \(P(24)\): \(24 = 4 \cdot 6\), so \(24\) can be formed using four \(6\)-cent stamps.
      \end{itemize}
      Therefore, all base cases are true.
      
      \textbf{Inductive step:} It will be proved that for any integer \(k \geq 24\), if \(P(m)\) is true for all integers \(m\) such that \(20 \leq m \leq k\), then \(P(k+1)\) is true.
      Suppose that \(P(m)\) is true for all integers \(m\) such that \(20 \leq m \leq k\), where \(k \geq 24\).
      It will be proved that \(P(k+1)\) is true, that is, \(k+1\) cents of postage can be formed using just \(5\)-cent and \(6\)-cent stamps.
      
      Since \(k \geq 24\), it follows that \(k+1 \geq 25\), so \((k+1) - 5 = k - 4 \geq 20\).
      Since \(k - 4 \geq 20\) and \(k - 4 \leq k\), by the inductive hypothesis, \(P(k-4)\) is true.
      Therefore, \(k-4\) cents can be formed using some combination of \(5\)-cent and \(6\)-cent stamps.
      By adding one \(5\)-cent stamp to the combination that forms \(k-4\) cents, \(k+1 = (k-4) + 5\) cents can be formed.
      Therefore, \(P(k+1)\) is true.
      
      By \nameref{def:strong-induction}, it follows that \(P(n)\) is true for all integers \(n \geq 20\).
      
      Therefore, all amounts of postage \(n \geq 20\) can be formed using just \(5\)-cent and \(6\)-cent stamps.
    \end{subprove}
  \end{enumerate}
  \item Let \(h\) be a real number with \(h > -1\).
  Prove that \(1 + n h \leq (1 + h)^{n}\) for all \(n \in \setZ\) with \(n > 0\).
  This is called Bernoulli's inequality.
  \begin{subprove}
    It will be proved that \(1 + n h \leq (1 + h)^{n}\) for all positive integers \(n\) and all real numbers \(h > -1\).
    
    Let \(P(n)\) be the proposition that \(1 + n h \leq (1 + h)^{n}\) for all real numbers \(h > -1\).
    
    \textbf{Base case:} For \(n = 1\), the left-hand side is \(1 + h\), and the right-hand side is \(1 + h\).
    Therefore, \(1 + h \leq 1 + h\), so \(P(1)\) is true.
    
    \textbf{Inductive step:} Suppose that \(P(k)\) is true for some positive integer \(k\).
    That is, suppose that \(1 + k h \leq (1 + h)^{k}\) for all real numbers \(h > -1\).
    It will be proved that \(P(k+1)\) is true.
    That is, it will be proved that \(1 + (k+1) h \leq (1 + h)^{k+1}\) for all real numbers \(h > -1\).
    
    Let \(h > -1\).
    By the inductive hypothesis, \(1 + k h \leq (1 + h)^{k}\).
    Since \(1 + h > 0\), multiplying both sides by \(1 + h\) yields \((1 + k h)(1 + h) \leq (1 + h)^{k+1}\).
    Expanding the left-hand side, \((1 + k h)(1 + h) = 1 + h + k h + k h^{2} = 1 + (k+1) h + k h^{2}\).
    Since \(k h^{2} \geq 0\), it follows that \(1 + (k+1) h + k h^{2} \geq 1 + (k+1) h\).
    Therefore, \(1 + (k+1) h \leq (1 + k h)(1 + h) \leq (1 + h)^{k+1}\).
    
    Therefore, \(P(k+1)\) is true.
    
    By \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}, it follows that \(P(n)\) is true for all positive integers \(n\).
  \end{subprove}
  \item Fix \(a, r \in \setR\).
  A \textbf{geometric progression} is an infinite sequence of the form \(a, a r, a r^{2}, \ldots, a r^{n}, \ldots\).
  Find a formula for the sum of the first \(n\) terms of a geometric progression, and prove it is correct using \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}.
  \begin{subprove}
    It will be proved that the sum of the first \(n\) terms of a geometric progression is \(\frac{a(r^{n} - 1)}{r - 1}\) when \(r \neq 1\), and \(n a\) when \(r = 1\).
    
    Let \(S_{n} = a + a r + a r^{2} + \ldots + a r^{n-1}\) be the sum of the first \(n\) terms.
    
    If \(r = 1\), then \(S_{n} = a + a + \ldots + a = n a\).
    
    If \(r \neq 1\), let \(P(n)\) be the proposition that \(S_{n} = \frac{a(r^{n} - 1)}{r - 1}\).
    
    \textbf{Base case:} For \(n = 1\), the left-hand side is \(S_{1} = a\), and the right-hand side is \(\frac{a(r^{1} - 1)}{r - 1} = \frac{a(r - 1)}{r - 1} = a\).
    Therefore, \(P(1)\) is true.
    
    \textbf{Inductive step:} Suppose that \(P(k)\) is true for some positive integer \(k\).
    That is, suppose that \(S_{k} = \frac{a(r^{k} - 1)}{r - 1}\).
    It will be proved that \(P(k+1)\) is true.
    That is, it will be proved that \(S_{k+1} = \frac{a(r^{k+1} - 1)}{r - 1}\).
    
    By definition, \(S_{k+1} = S_{k} + a r^{k}\).
    By the inductive hypothesis, \(S_{k} = \frac{a(r^{k} - 1)}{r - 1}\).
    Therefore, \(S_{k+1} = \frac{a(r^{k} - 1)}{r - 1} + a r^{k} = \frac{a(r^{k} - 1) + a r^{k}(r - 1)}{r - 1} = \frac{a r^{k} - a + a r^{k+1} - a r^{k}}{r - 1} = \frac{a r^{k+1} - a}{r - 1} = \frac{a(r^{k+1} - 1)}{r - 1}\).
    
    Therefore, \(P(k+1)\) is true.
    
    By \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}, it follows that \(P(n)\) is true for all positive integers \(n\).
  \end{subprove}
\end{enumerate}






\newpage
\begin{define}{Binomial Coefficient}[def:binomial-coefficient][red]
  For nonnegative integers \(n\) and \(k\) with \(k \leq n\), the \textbf{binomial coefficient} \(\binom{n}{k}\) is defined by
  \[
  \binom{n}{k} = \frac{n!}{k!(n-k)!}.
  \]
\end{define}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item Let \(h\) be a real number with \(h > -1\).
  Prove that \(1 + n h \leq (1 + h)^{n}\) for all \(n \in \setZ\) with \(n > 0\).
  This is called Bernoulli's inequality.
  \begin{subprove}
    It will be proved that \(1 + n h \leq (1 + h)^{n}\) for all positive integers \(n\) and all real numbers \(h > -1\).
    
    Let \(P(n)\) be the proposition that \(1 + n h \leq (1 + h)^{n}\) for all real numbers \(h > -1\).
    
    \textbf{Base case:} For \(n = 1\), the left-hand side is \(1 + h\), and the right-hand side is \(1 + h\).
    Therefore, \(1 + h \leq 1 + h\), so \(P(1)\) is true.
    
    \textbf{Inductive step:} Suppose that \(P(k)\) is true for some positive integer \(k\).
    That is, suppose that \(1 + k h \leq (1 + h)^{k}\) for all real numbers \(h > -1\).
    It will be proved that \(P(k+1)\) is true.
    That is, it will be proved that \(1 + (k+1) h \leq (1 + h)^{k+1}\) for all real numbers \(h > -1\).
    
    Let \(h > -1\).
    By the inductive hypothesis, \(1 + k h \leq (1 + h)^{k}\).
    Since \(1 + h > 0\), multiplying both sides by \(1 + h\) yields \((1 + k h)(1 + h) \leq (1 + h)^{k+1}\).
    Expanding the left-hand side, \((1 + k h)(1 + h) = 1 + h + k h + k h^{2} = 1 + (k+1) h + k h^{2}\).
    Since \(k h^{2} \geq 0\), it follows that \(1 + (k+1) h + k h^{2} \geq 1 + (k+1) h\).
    Therefore, \(1 + (k+1) h \leq (1 + k h)(1 + h) \leq (1 + h)^{k+1}\).
    
    Therefore, \(P(k+1)\) is true.
    
    By \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}, it follows that \(P(n)\) is true for all positive integers \(n\).
  \end{subprove}
  \item Fix \(a, r \in \setR\).
  A \textbf{geometric progression} is an infinite sequence of the form \(a, a r, a r^{2}, \ldots, a r^{n}, \ldots\).
  Find a formula for the sum of the first \(n\) terms of a geometric progression, and prove it is correct using \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}.
  \begin{subprove}
    It will be proved that the sum of the first \(n\) terms of a geometric progression is \(\frac{a(r^{n} - 1)}{r - 1}\) when \(r \neq 1\), and \(n a\) when \(r = 1\).
    
    Let \(S_{n} = a + a r + a r^{2} + \ldots + a r^{n-1}\) be the sum of the first \(n\) terms.
    
    If \(r = 1\), then \(S_{n} = a + a + \ldots + a = n a\).
    
    If \(r \neq 1\), let \(P(n)\) be the proposition that \(S_{n} = \frac{a(r^{n} - 1)}{r - 1}\).
    
    \textbf{Base case:} For \(n = 1\), the left-hand side is \(S_{1} = a\), and the right-hand side is \(\frac{a(r^{1} - 1)}{r - 1} = \frac{a(r - 1)}{r - 1} = a\).
    Therefore, \(P(1)\) is true.
    
    \textbf{Inductive step:} Suppose that \(P(k)\) is true for some positive integer \(k\).
    That is, suppose that \(S_{k} = \frac{a(r^{k} - 1)}{r - 1}\).
    It will be proved that \(P(k+1)\) is true.
    That is, it will be proved that \(S_{k+1} = \frac{a(r^{k+1} - 1)}{r - 1}\).
    
    By definition, \(S_{k+1} = S_{k} + a r^{k}\).
    By the inductive hypothesis, \(S_{k} = \frac{a(r^{k} - 1)}{r - 1}\).
    Therefore, \(S_{k+1} = \frac{a(r^{k} - 1)}{r - 1} + a r^{k} = \frac{a(r^{k} - 1) + a r^{k}(r - 1)}{r - 1} = \frac{a r^{k} - a + a r^{k+1} - a r^{k}}{r - 1} = \frac{a r^{k+1} - a}{r - 1} = \frac{a(r^{k+1} - 1)}{r - 1}\).
    
    Therefore, \(P(k+1)\) is true.
    
    By \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}, it follows that \(P(n)\) is true for all positive integers \(n\).
  \end{subprove}
  \item Prove by \nameref{def:mathematical-induction} that a set having \(n\) elements has exactly \(2^{n}\) subsets.
  \begin{subprove}
    It will be proved that a set having \(n\) elements has exactly \(2^{n}\) subsets for all nonnegative integers \(n\).
    
    Let \(P(n)\) be the proposition that a set having \(n\) elements has exactly \(2^{n}\) subsets.
    
    \textbf{Base case:} For \(n = 0\), the empty set has exactly one subset, namely itself, and \(2^{0} = 1\).
    Therefore, \(P(0)\) is true.
    
    For \(n = 1\), a set with one element has exactly two subsets: the empty set and the set itself, and \(2^{1} = 2\).
    Therefore, \(P(1)\) is true.
    
    \textbf{Inductive step:} Suppose that \(P(k)\) is true for some nonnegative integer \(k \geq 1\).
    That is, suppose that a set having \(k\) elements has exactly \(2^{k}\) subsets.
    It will be proved that \(P(k+1)\) is true.
    That is, it will be proved that a set having \(k+1\) elements has exactly \(2^{k+1}\) subsets.
    
    Let \(S\) be a set with \(k+1\) elements, and let \(x \in S\).
    Consider the set \(S' = S \setminus \{x\}\), which has \(k\) elements.
    By the inductive hypothesis, \(S'\) has exactly \(2^{k}\) subsets.
    The subsets of \(S\) can be partitioned into two classes: those that do not contain \(x\) and those that do contain \(x\).
    The subsets that do not contain \(x\) are exactly the subsets of \(S'\), so there are \(2^{k}\) of them.
    The subsets that do contain \(x\) are of the form \(A \cup \{x\}\), where \(A\) is a subset of \(S'\), so there are also \(2^{k}\) of them.
    Therefore, the total number of subsets of \(S\) is \(2^{k} + 2^{k} = 2 \cdot 2^{k} = 2^{k+1}\).
    
    Therefore, \(P(k+1)\) is true.
    
    By \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}, it follows that \(P(n)\) is true for all nonnegative integers \(n\).
  \end{subprove}
  \item Prove by \nameref{def:mathematical-induction} on \(k\) that a set of size \(n\) has exactly \(\binom{n}{k}\) subsets of size \(k\), where \(\binom{n}{k}\) is defined in \nameref{def:binomial-coefficient}.
  \begin{subprove}
    It will be proved that a set of size \(n\) has exactly \(\binom{n}{k}\) subsets of size \(k\) for all nonnegative integers \(n\) and \(k\) with \(k \leq n\).
    
    Let \(P(k)\) be the proposition that a set of size \(n\) has exactly \(\binom{n}{k}\) subsets of size \(k\) for all \(n \geq k\).
    
    \textbf{Base case:} For \(k = 0\), a set of any size has exactly one subset of size \(0\), namely the empty set, and \(\binom{n}{0} = \frac{n!}{0!(n-0)!} = \frac{n!}{n!} = 1\).
    Therefore, \(P(0)\) is true.
    
    \textbf{Inductive step:} Suppose that \(P(k)\) is true for some nonnegative integer \(k\).
    That is, suppose that a set of size \(n\) has exactly \(\binom{n}{k}\) subsets of size \(k\) for all \(n \geq k\).
    It will be proved that \(P(k+1)\) is true.
    That is, it will be proved that a set of size \(n\) has exactly \(\binom{n}{k+1}\) subsets of size \(k+1\) for all \(n \geq k+1\).
    
    Let \(S\) be a set with \(n \geq k+1\) elements, and let \(x \in S\).
    Consider the set \(S' = S \setminus \{x\}\), which has \(n-1\) elements.
    The subsets of \(S\) of size \(k+1\) can be partitioned into two classes: those that do not contain \(x\) and those that do contain \(x\).
    The subsets that do not contain \(x\) are exactly the subsets of \(S'\) of size \(k+1\), so by the inductive hypothesis, there are \(\binom{n-1}{k+1}\) of them.
    The subsets that do contain \(x\) are of the form \(A \cup \{x\}\), where \(A\) is a subset of \(S'\) of size \(k\), so by the inductive hypothesis, there are \(\binom{n-1}{k}\) of them.
    Therefore, the total number of subsets of \(S\) of size \(k+1\) is \(\binom{n-1}{k+1} + \binom{n-1}{k}\).
    By the recurrence relation for binomial coefficients, \(\binom{n-1}{k+1} + \binom{n-1}{k} = \binom{n}{k+1}\).
    Therefore, a set of size \(n\) has exactly \(\binom{n}{k+1}\) subsets of size \(k+1\).
    
    Therefore, \(P(k+1)\) is true.
    
    By \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}, it follows that \(P(k)\) is true for all nonnegative integers \(k\).
  \end{subprove}
  \item Let \(m > 1\) be an integer and \(H_{m} = \{m x \mid x \in \setZ\}\).
  Prove that \(H_{m}\) is a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \((\setZ, +)\).
  \begin{subprove}
    Let \(m > 1\) be an integer, let \(H_{m} = \{m x \mid x \in \setZ\}\), and let \(G = (\setZ, +)\).
    It will be proved that \(H_{m}\) is a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \(G\).
    By \nameref{def:subgroup-lemma}, it suffices to show that (1) \(H_{m} \neq \emptyset\), (2) for all \(a, b \in H_{m}\), \(a + b \in H_{m}\), and (3) for all \(a \in H_{m}\), \(-a \in H_{m}\).
    \begin{itemize}
      \item Since \(m \cdot 0 = 0 \in H_{m}\), it follows that \(H_{m} \neq \emptyset\).
      \item For any \(a, b \in H_{m}\), there exist \(x, y \in \setZ\) such that \(a = m x\) and \(b = m y\).
      Therefore, \(a + b = m x + m y = m(x + y) \in H_{m}\).
      \item For any \(a \in H_{m}\), there exists \(x \in \setZ\) such that \(a = m x\).
      Therefore, \(-a = -(m x) = m(-x) \in H_{m}\).
    \end{itemize}
    Therefore, \(H_{m}\) is a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \((\setZ, +)\).
  \end{subprove}
  \item Let \(G\) be a \nameref{def:group} with identity \(e\) and \(H = \{a \in G \mid a^{2} = e\}\).
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item Prove that if \(G\) is \nameref{def:abeliangroup}, then \(H\) is a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \(G\).
    \begin{subprove}
      Let \(G\) be a \nameref{def:group} with identity \(e\), let \(H = \{a \in G \mid a^{2} = e\}\), and suppose that \(G\) is \nameref{def:abeliangroup}.
      It will be proved that \(H\) is a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \(G\).
      By \nameref{def:subgroup-lemma}, it suffices to show that (1) \(H \neq \emptyset\), (2) for all \(a, b \in H\), \(a * b \in H\), and (3) for all \(a \in H\), \(a^{-1} \in H\).
      \begin{itemize}
        \item Since \(e^{2} = e\), it follows that \(e \in H\), so \(H \neq \emptyset\).
        \item For any \(a, b \in H\), since \(a^{2} = e\) and \(b^{2} = e\), and since \(G\) is \nameref{def:abeliangroup}, \((a * b)^{2} = (a * b) * (a * b) = a * b * a * b = a * a * b * b = a^{2} * b^{2} = e * e = e\).
        Therefore, \(a * b \in H\).
        \item For any \(a \in H\), since \(a^{2} = e\), it follows that \(a = a^{-1}\).
        Therefore, \((a^{-1})^{2} = a^{2} = e\), so \(a^{-1} \in H\).
      \end{itemize}
      Therefore, \(H\) is a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \(G\).
    \end{subprove}
    \item Is the statement true if \(G\) is nonabelian? Justify your answer.
    \begin{subprove}
      It will be proved that the statement is false if \(G\) is nonabelian.
      A counterexample will be provided.
      
      Let \(G = S_{3}\), the symmetric group of degree \(3\), which is a nonabelian \nameref{def:group} as shown earlier.
      Let \(H = \{a \in S_{3} \mid a^{2} = e\}\), where \(e = i\) is the identity permutation.
      It will be proved that \(H\) is not a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \(S_{3}\).
      
      First, the elements of \(H\) will be determined.
      The elements of \(S_{3}\) are \(i, f, g, g^{2}, f g, g f\), where:
      \begin{itemize}
        \item \(i = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 \\ 1 & 2 & 3 \end{pmatrix}\) is the identity permutation.
        \item \(f = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 \\ 2 & 1 & 3 \end{pmatrix}\).
        \item \(g = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 \\ 2 & 3 & 1 \end{pmatrix}\).
      \end{itemize}
      
      It will be checked which elements satisfy \(a^{2} = i\):
      \begin{itemize}
        \item \(i^{2} = i\), so \(i \in H\).
        \item \(f^{2} = i\) (as shown earlier), so \(f \in H\).
        \item \(g^{2} \neq i\) (since \(g^{3} = i\) and \(g \neq i\)), so \(g \notin H\).
        \item \((g^{2})^{2} = g^{4} = g^{3} \cdot g = i \cdot g = g \neq i\), so \(g^{2} \notin H\).
        \item \((f g)^{2} = i\) (as shown earlier, since \(f g\) is a reflection), so \(f g \in H\).
        \item \((g f)^{2} = i\) (as shown earlier, since \(g f\) is a reflection), so \(g f \in H\).
      \end{itemize}
      
      Therefore, \(H = \{i, f, f g, g f\}\).
      
      Now, it will be checked whether \(H\) is closed under composition.
      From the product table for \(S_{3}\) computed earlier, \(f \cdot (f g) = g\).
      Since \(g^{2} \neq i\), it follows that \(g \notin H\).
      Therefore, \(f \in H\) and \(f g \in H\), but \(f \cdot (f g) = g \notin H\).
      Therefore, \(H\) is not closed under composition.
      
      Since \(H\) is not closed under composition, it follows that \(H\) is not a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \(S_{3}\).
      
      Therefore, the statement that \(H = \{a \in G \mid a^{2} = e\}\) is a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \(G\) is false when \(G\) is nonabelian.
    \end{subprove}
  \end{enumerate}
  \item Let \((G, *)\) be a \nameref{def:group} and \(H\) a nonempty subset of \(G\) that is closed under \(*\).
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item Prove that if \(H\) is finite, then \(H\) is a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \(G\).
    \begin{subprove}
      Let \((G, *)\) be a \nameref{def:group} with identity \(e\), let \(H\) be a nonempty finite subset of \(G\) that is closed under \(*\).
      It will be proved that \(H\) is a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \(G\).
      By \nameref{def:subgroup-lemma}, it suffices to show that for all \(a \in H\), \(a^{-1} \in H\).
      Let \(a \in H\).
      Since \(H\) is closed under \(*\) and finite, the sequence \(a, a^{2}, a^{3}, \ldots\) must eventually repeat.
      That is, there exist positive integers \(m < n\) such that \(a^{m} = a^{n}\).
      Since \(a^{m} = a^{n}\), multiplying both sides by \(a^{-m}\) on the right yields \(a^{m} * a^{-m} = a^{n} * a^{-m}\), which simplifies to \(e = a^{n-m}\).
      Since \(n - m > 0\), it follows that \(a^{n-m-1} * a = e\), so \(a^{n-m-1} = a^{-1}\).
      Since \(H\) is closed under \(*\), \(a^{n-m-1} \in H\), so \(a^{-1} \in H\).
      Therefore, \(H\) is a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \(G\).
    \end{subprove}
    \item Is \(H\) a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \(G\) if \(G\) is finite? Justify your answer.
    \begin{subprove}
      It will be proved that if \(G\) is finite, then \(H\) is a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \(G\).
      
      Suppose that \(G\) is finite.
      Since \(H\) is a subset of \(G\) and \(G\) is finite, it follows that \(H\) is finite.
      Since \(H\) is nonempty, finite, and closed under \(*\), by the previous result, it follows that \(H\) is a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \(G\).
      
      Therefore, if \(G\) is finite, then \(H\) is a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \(G\).
    \end{subprove}
    \item What about if \(G\) is infinite? Justify your answer.
    \begin{subprove}
      It will be proved that if \(G\) is infinite, then \(H\) is not necessarily a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \(G\).
      A counterexample will be provided.
      
      If \(H\) is finite, then by the previous result, \(H\) is a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \(G\).
      However, if \(H\) is infinite, then \(H\) might not be a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \(G\).
      
      A counterexample will be provided.
      Let \(G = (\setZ, +)\), the group of integers under addition, which is infinite.
      Let \(H = \{0, 1, 2, 3, \ldots\}\), the set of nonnegative integers.
      
      It will be checked whether \(H\) satisfies the conditions:
      \begin{itemize}
        \item \(H\) is nonempty since \(0 \in H\).
        \item \(H\) is closed under addition since the sum of two nonnegative integers is a nonnegative integer.
      \end{itemize}
      
      However, \(H\) is not a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \(G\).
      By \nameref{def:subgroup-lemma}, a subset \(H \subseteq G\) is a \nameref{def:subgroup} if and only if (1) \(H \neq \emptyset\), (2) for all \(a, b \in H\), \(a + b \in H\), and (3) for all \(a \in H\), \(-a \in H\).
      While \(H\) satisfies conditions (1) and (2), it does not satisfy condition (3).
      For example, \(1 \in H\), but \(-1 \notin H\).
      Therefore, \(H\) is not a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \((\setZ, +)\).
      
      Therefore, if \(G\) is infinite, then \(H\) is not necessarily a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \(G\).
      However, if \(H\) is finite (even when \(G\) is infinite), then \(H\) is a \nameref{def:subgroup} of \(G\) by the previous result.
    \end{subprove}
  \end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}







\newpage
\begin{define}{Equivalence Relation}[def:equivalence-relation][red]
  Let \(S\) be a nonempty set.
  A relation \(R\) on \(S\) is called an \textbf{equivalence relation} if (and only if) it satisfies:
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Reflexivity:} For all \(a \in S\), \((a, a) \in R\).
    \item \textbf{Symmetry:} For all \(a, b \in S\), if \((a, b) \in R\), then \((b, a) \in R\).
    \item \textbf{Transitivity:} For all \(a, b, c \in S\), if \((a, b) \in R\) and \((b, c) \in R\), then \((a, c) \in R\).
  \end{enumerate}
\end{define}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item Let \((G, *)\) and \((H, \circ)\) be two \nameref{def:group}s.
  Prove that if \(\phi: G \to H\) is a \nameref{def:group-isomorphism}, then the inverse function \(\phi^{-1}\) is a \nameref{def:group-isomorphism} from \(H\) to \(G\).
  \begin{subprove}
    Let \((G, *)\) and \((H, \circ)\) be \nameref{def:group}s, and let \(\phi: G \to H\) be a \nameref{def:group-isomorphism}.
    It will be proved that \(\phi^{-1}: H \to G\) is a \nameref{def:group-isomorphism}.
    
    Since \(\phi\) is a bijection, \(\phi^{-1}\) is also a bijection.
    It remains to show that \(\phi^{-1}\) preserves the operation.
    That is, it will be proved that \(\phi^{-1}(h_{1} \circ h_{2}) = \phi^{-1}(h_{1}) * \phi^{-1}(h_{2})\) for all \(h_{1}, h_{2} \in H\).
    
    Let \(h_{1}, h_{2} \in H\).
    Since \(\phi\) is surjective, there exist \(g_{1}, g_{2} \in G\) such that \(\phi(g_{1}) = h_{1}\) and \(\phi(g_{2}) = h_{2}\).
    Since \(\phi\) is injective, \(g_{1} = \phi^{-1}(h_{1})\) and \(g_{2} = \phi^{-1}(h_{2})\).
    Since \(\phi\) preserves the operation, \(\phi(g_{1} * g_{2}) = \phi(g_{1}) \circ \phi(g_{2}) = h_{1} \circ h_{2}\).
    Therefore, \(\phi^{-1}(h_{1} \circ h_{2}) = g_{1} * g_{2} = \phi^{-1}(h_{1}) * \phi^{-1}(h_{2})\).
    
    Therefore, \(\phi^{-1}\) is a \nameref{def:group-isomorphism}.
  \end{subprove}
  \item Let \(G, H\), and \(K\) be \nameref{def:group}s.
  Prove that if \(\phi: G \to H\) and \(\psi: H \to K\) are \nameref{def:group-isomorphism}s, then the composition \(\psi \circ \phi: G \to K\) is a \nameref{def:group-isomorphism}.
  \begin{subprove}
    Let \(G, H\), and \(K\) be \nameref{def:group}s, and let \(\phi: G \to H\) and \(\psi: H \to K\) be \nameref{def:group-isomorphism}s.
    It will be proved that \(\psi \circ \phi: G \to K\) is a \nameref{def:group-isomorphism}.
    
    Since \(\phi\) and \(\psi\) are bijections, their composition \(\psi \circ \phi\) is also a bijection.
    It remains to show that \(\psi \circ \phi\) preserves the operation.
    That is, it will be proved that \((\psi \circ \phi)(g_{1} * g_{2}) = (\psi \circ \phi)(g_{1}) \star (\psi \circ \phi)(g_{2})\) for all \(g_{1}, g_{2} \in G\), where \(\star\) is the operation on \(K\).
    
    Let \(g_{1}, g_{2} \in G\).
    Since \(\phi\) preserves the operation, \(\phi(g_{1} * g_{2}) = \phi(g_{1}) \circ \phi(g_{2})\), where \(\circ\) is the operation on \(H\).
    Since \(\psi\) preserves the operation, \(\psi(\phi(g_{1}) \circ \phi(g_{2})) = \psi(\phi(g_{1})) \star \psi(\phi(g_{2}))\).
    Therefore, \((\psi \circ \phi)(g_{1} * g_{2}) = \psi(\phi(g_{1} * g_{2})) = \psi(\phi(g_{1}) \circ \phi(g_{2})) = \psi(\phi(g_{1})) \star \psi(\phi(g_{2})) = (\psi \circ \phi)(g_{1}) \star (\psi \circ \phi)(g_{2})\).
    
    Therefore, \(\psi \circ \phi\) is a \nameref{def:group-isomorphism}.
  \end{subprove}
  \item Let \(\mathcal{G}\) be the set of all \nameref{def:group}s.
  Define a relation on \(\mathcal{G}\) where \(G \simeq G'\) if and only if \(G\) is isomorphic to \(G'\) for all \(G, G' \in \mathcal{G}\).
  Prove that this is an \nameref{def:equivalence-relation}.
  \begin{subprove}
    Let \(\mathcal{G}\) be the set of all \nameref{def:group}s, and let \(R\) be the relation on \(\mathcal{G}\) defined by \((G, G') \in R\) if and only if \(G \simeq G'\).
    It will be proved that \(R\) is an \nameref{def:equivalence-relation}.
    
    \textbf{Reflexivity:} For any \(G \in \mathcal{G}\), the identity map \(\text{id}_{G}: G \to G\) defined by \(\text{id}_{G}(g) = g\) for all \(g \in G\) is a \nameref{def:group-isomorphism}.
    Therefore, \(G \simeq G\), so \((G, G) \in R\).
    
    \textbf{Symmetry:} For any \(G, G' \in \mathcal{G}\), if \((G, G') \in R\), then \(G \simeq G'\), so there exists a \nameref{def:group-isomorphism} \(\phi: G \to G'\).
    By the previous problem, \(\phi^{-1}: G' \to G\) is also a \nameref{def:group-isomorphism}, so \(G' \simeq G\).
    Therefore, \((G', G) \in R\).
    
    \textbf{Transitivity:} For any \(G, G', G'' \in \mathcal{G}\), if \((G, G') \in R\) and \((G', G'') \in R\), then \(G \simeq G'\) and \(G' \simeq G''\), so there exist \nameref{def:group-isomorphism}s \(\phi: G \to G'\) and \(\psi: G' \to G''\).
    By the previous problem, \(\psi \circ \phi: G \to G''\) is also a \nameref{def:group-isomorphism}, so \(G \simeq G''\).
    Therefore, \((G, G'') \in R\).
    
    Since \(R\) satisfies reflexivity, symmetry, and transitivity, it follows that \(R\) is an \nameref{def:equivalence-relation}.
  \end{subprove}
  \item Let \(m\) be a positive integer.
  Prove that if \(a\) and \(b\) are integers with \(a \equiv b \pmod{m}\), then \(a^{k} \equiv b^{k} \pmod{m}\) whenever \(k\) is a nonnegative integer.
  \begin{subprove}
    Let \(m\) be a positive integer, let \(a, b \in \setZ\) such that \(a \equiv b \pmod{m}\), and let \(k\) be a nonnegative integer.
    It will be proved that \(a^{k} \equiv b^{k} \pmod{m}\).
    
    Let \(P(k)\) be the proposition that \(a^{k} \equiv b^{k} \pmod{m}\).
    
    \textbf{Base case:} For \(k = 0\), \(a^{0} = 1 = b^{0}\), so \(a^{0} \equiv b^{0} \pmod{m}\).
    Therefore, \(P(0)\) is true.
    
    \textbf{Inductive step:} Suppose that \(P(k)\) is true for some nonnegative integer \(k\).
    That is, suppose that \(a^{k} \equiv b^{k} \pmod{m}\).
    It will be proved that \(P(k+1)\) is true.
    That is, it will be proved that \(a^{k+1} \equiv b^{k+1} \pmod{m}\).
    
    By the inductive hypothesis, \(a^{k} \equiv b^{k} \pmod{m}\).
    By assumption, \(a \equiv b \pmod{m}\).
    Since congruence modulo \(m\) is preserved under multiplication, \(a^{k} \cdot a \equiv b^{k} \cdot b \pmod{m}\).
    Therefore, \(a^{k+1} \equiv b^{k+1} \pmod{m}\).
    
    Therefore, \(P(k+1)\) is true.
    
    By \nameref{def:mathematical-induction}, it follows that \(P(k)\) is true for all nonnegative integers \(k\).
    Therefore, \(a^{k} \equiv b^{k} \pmod{m}\) whenever \(k\) is a nonnegative integer.
  \end{subprove}
  \item Let \(G\) be a set with an operation \(*\) such that:
  \begin{itemize}
    \item \(G\) is closed under \(*\).
    \item \(*\) is associative.
    \item There exists an element \(e \in G\) such that \(e * x = x\) for all \(x \in G\).
    \item Given \(x \in G\), there exists a \(y \in G\) such that \(y * x = e\).
  \end{itemize}
  Prove that \(G\) is a \nameref{def:group}.
  \begin{subprove}
    Let \(G\) be a set with an operation \(*\) satisfying the given conditions.
    It will be proved that \(G\) is a \nameref{def:group}.
    
    By the given conditions, \(G\) is closed under \(*\), \(*\) is associative, and there exists an identity element \(e\) such that \(e * x = x\) for all \(x \in G\).
    It remains to show that \(e\) is also a right identity and that every element has a right inverse.
    
    First, it will be proved that the left inverse of \(x\) is also a right inverse.
    Let \(x \in G\).
    By the given condition, there exists \(y \in G\) such that \(y * x = e\).
    It will be proved that \(x * y = e\).
    
    Since \(y * x = e\), and since \(y\) has a left inverse, there exists \(z \in G\) such that \(z * y = e\).
    It will be proved that \(z = x\).
    
    Since \(z * y = e\) and \(y * x = e\), it follows that \(z = z * e = z * (y * x) = (z * y) * x = e * x = x\).
    Therefore, \(z = x\).
    
    Since \(z * y = e\) and \(z = x\), it follows that \(x * y = e\).
    
    Therefore, the left inverse \(y\) of \(x\) is also a right inverse of \(x\).
    
    Now, it will be proved that \(e\) is a right identity.
    That is, it will be proved that \(x * e = x\) for all \(x \in G\).
    
    Let \(x \in G\).
    By the given condition, there exists \(y \in G\) such that \(y * x = e\).
    As shown above, \(x * y = e\).
    Therefore, \(x * e = x * (y * x) = (x * y) * x = e * x = x\).
    
    Therefore, \(e\) is a right identity.
    
    Since \(G\) is closed under \(*\), \(*\) is associative, there exists an identity element \(e\) such that \(e * x = x * e = x\) for all \(x \in G\), and every element has both a left and right inverse, it follows that \(G\) is a \nameref{def:group}.
  \end{subprove}
\end{enumerate}

% Definition: A nonempty set $G$ is said to be a group under the binary operation * on $G$, denoted $(G, *)$, if (and only if) the following four axioms are satisfied:
% (1) Closure: For all $a, b \in G, a * b \in G$.
% (2) Associativity: For all $a, b, c \in G, a *(b * c)=(a * b) * c$.
% (3) Existence of an identity: There exists $e \in G$ such that $a * e=e * a=a$ for all $a \in G$.
% (4) Existence of inverses: For every $a \in G$ there exists $b \in G$ such that $a * b=b * a=e$.

% Groups are generalizations of the familiar products of addition and of multiplication of real numbers. Remember, though, that a set with a product is a group if and only if the four axioms are satisfied!

% For instance, $\setZ$ is a group under addition but not under multiplication (for more than one reason). The set $\setQ$ is also group under addition but not under multiplication. The set $\setR^{+}$of positive real numbers is a group under multiplication but not under addition.

% The groups above are infinite, but many interesting groups are finite. The order of a group $G$ is the number of elements in $G$, denoted $|\boldsymbol{G}|$. If (and only if) $|\boldsymbol{G}|<\infty$, then $G$ is a finite group.

% The smallest group consists of a single (identity) element, usually denoted $e$ (or sometimes $i$ when the group elements are functions).
% Here's an example of a group of order 2:
% Claim: The set $\{-1,1\}$ under multiplication is a group.
% Proof: Consider the set $\{-1,1\}$ under multiplication, denoted $\cdot$. Since $-1 \cdot-1=1=1 \cdot 1$ and $-1 \cdot 1=-1=1 \cdot-1$, then $\{-1,1\}$ is closed under multiplication. Since multiplication of real number is associative, then multiplication on $\{-1,1\}$ is associative. Since $-1 \cdot 1=1 \cdot-1=-1$ and $1 \cdot 1=1$, then 1 is the identity under multiplication. Since $-1 \cdot-1=1$ and $1 \cdot 1=1$, then both -1 and 1 have multiplicative inverses. Since all four axioms are satisfied, $(\{-1,1\}, \cdot)$ is a group. QED

% When the order of a group is small, it is often very useful to write out the product table for the group.


% For instance, let $G$ be a group of order two with product $*$. Then $G=\{e, a\}$ where $e$ is the identity and $a$ denotes the single non-identity element. The product table for $G$ is:

% \begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
% \hline$*$ & $e$ & $a$ \\
% \hline$e$ & $e$ & $a$ \\
% \hline$a$ & $a$ & $e$ \\
% \hline
% \end{tabular}

% This is the "only" group of order 2. By which we mean that all groups of order 2 must be isomorphic to this group.
% Definition: Let $(G, *)$ and $\left(G^{\prime}, \star\right)$ be two groups with products * and * respectively. We say that $G$ is isomorphic to $G^{\prime}$, and write $\boldsymbol{G} \simeq \boldsymbol{G}^{\prime}$, if (and only if) there exists a function $\phi: G \rightarrow G^{\prime}$ such that
% (1) $\phi$ is a bijection, and
% (2) $\phi(a * b)=\phi(a) \star \phi(b)$.

% Claim: The group $(\{-1,1\}, \cdot)$ is isomorphic to the group $(G, *)$ with product table above.
% Proof: Let $\phi:\{-1,1\} \rightarrow\{e, a\}$ be the function given by $\phi(1)=e$ and $\phi(-1)=a$. Then by inspection $\phi$ is one-to-one and onto, and hence a bijection. Also, $\phi(1 \cdot 1)=\phi(1)=e=e * e=\phi(1) * \phi(1)$, $\phi(1 \cdot-1)=\phi(-1)=a=e * a=\phi(1) * \phi(-1)$, $\phi(-1 \cdot 1)=\phi(-1)=a=a * e=\phi(-1) * \phi(1)$, and $\phi(-1 \cdot-1)=\phi(1)=e=a * a=\phi(-1) * \phi(-1)$.
% Hence, $\phi$ preserve operations, and $(\{-1,1\}, \cdot) \simeq(\{e, a\}, *)$. QED
% Can you think of another group of order 2 ? What about the set $\left\{[0]_2,[1]_2\right\}$ of congrunence classes modulo two under addition? Can you prove this is isomorphic to $(G, *)$ with product table above?

% \section*{Overview}
% The announcement asks you to practice the \textbf{Goldilocks principle} in mathematical writing: give \emph{just the right amount} of explanation — not too little and not too much. These notes explain what that means, give short course-related examples, a checklist you can use in class or on exams, and practical tips for practicing the skill.

% \section{What the announcement means (short)}
% \begin{itemize}
%   \item \textbf{Too little:} You omit necessary steps or justification; the reader cannot follow or verify your reasoning.
%   \item \textbf{Too much:} You repeat or restate obvious facts, or include irrelevant digressions that bury the main point.
%   \item \textbf{Just right:} Provide the definitions you need, the logical steps that connect them, and a clear conclusion — compact, precise, and sufficient for the intended audience to verify the result.
% \end{itemize}

% \section{Why it matters beyond exams}
% \begin{itemize}
%   \item \emph{Homework \& assignments:} graders need enough to see you understood the idea — but not long-winded prose.
%   \item \emph{Research / papers:} reviewers want clear, rigorous proofs and concise motivation.
%   \item \emph{Coding / code review:} succinct comments and correct edge-case handling beat verbose, noisy comments.
%   \item \emph{Presentations / teaching:} clarity and economy keep the audience engaged.
%   \item \emph{Professional writing:} concise but sufficient detail (design docs, emails, bug reports) leads to faster, less-error-prone collaboration.
% \end{itemize}

% \section{Concrete course examples}
% Below are short ``too little / too much / just right'' style model answers for typical course problems.

% \subsection{Equivalence relation}
% \begin{define}{Relation defined by divisibility}
% Let \(R\) be a relation on \(\setZ\) defined by
% \[
% a \,R\, b \IFF 3 \mid (a-b).
% \]
% Show that \(R\) is an equivalence relation.
% \end{define}

% \paragraph{Just-right solution:}
% \begin{subprove}
%   \textbf{Reflexive.} For any \(a\in\setZ\), \(a-a=0\) and \(3\mid 0\); thus \(aRa\).
%   \textbf{Symmetric.} If \(3\mid (a-b)\) then \(3\mid (b-a)\), so \(aRb\Rightarrow bRa\).
%   \textbf{Transitive.} If \(3\mid (a-b)\) and \(3\mid (b-c)\), then \(3\mid (a-c)\); hence \(aRb\) and \(bRc\Rightarrow aRc\).
%   Therefore \(R\) is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive, i.e., an equivalence relation.
% \end{subprove}


% \begin{define}{Function: bijectivity}
% Let \(f:\setZ\to\setZ\) be defined by \(f(n)=n+1\). Prove \(f\) is bijective.
% \end{define}

% \paragraph{Just-right solution:}
% \begin{subprove}
% \textbf{Injective.} If \(f(a)=f(b)\) then \(a+1=b+1\), so \(a=b\).
% \textbf{Surjective.} For arbitrary \(y\in\setZ\) choose \(x=y-1\). Then \(f(x)=x+1=y\).
% Thus \(f\) is both injective and surjective, hence bijective.
% \end{subprove}


% \begin{define}{Partial order: subset relation}
% Show \((\mathcal{P}(X),\subseteq)\) is a partially ordered set.
% \end{define}

% \paragraph{Just-right solution:}
% \begin{subprove}
% \textbf{Reflexive.} For any \(A\subseteq X\), \(A\subseteq A\).
% \textbf{Antisymmetric.} If \(A\subseteq B\) and \(B\subseteq A\), then \(A=B\).
% \textbf{Transitive.} If \(A\subseteq B\) and \(B\subseteq C\), then \(A\subseteq C\).
% Hence \(\subseteq\) satisfies reflexivity, antisymmetry, and transitivity, so it is a partial order.
% \end{subprove}

% \section{A short checklist for ``Goldilocks'' answers}
% Before submitting or handing in a solution, run this mental checklist:
% \begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.]
%   \item \textbf{State what you are proving} in one crisp sentence.
%   \item \textbf{Give necessary definitions} (only those used in the argument).
%   \item \textbf{Outline the plan} if the proof is longer than a couple of lines.
%   \item \textbf{Do the steps:} each step should follow logically and be minimally justified.
%   \item \textbf{Address edge cases} when relevant (e.g., empty set, domain restrictions).
%   \item \textbf{Conclude clearly:} ``Hence ... is ...'' or similar.
%   \item \textbf{Edit for redundancy:} remove repeated or obvious statements.
% \end{enumerate}

% \section{Practical tips to practice this skill}
% \begin{itemize}
%   \item After writing a solution, \emph{try removing one sentence}. If the argument still works, that sentence was probably redundant; if it breaks, put it back.
%   \item Conversely, \emph{remove a justification line} — if the reader cannot fill the gap, restore the line.
%   \item When reviewing classmates' solutions, mark places that are unclear (too little) or repetitive (too much).
%   \item Time yourself: write a focused 5--7 line solution to a typical problem; this trains concise clarity.
% \end{itemize}

\textbf{Remark}: The following definitions (or results) are already proved in class or on previous ICA, 
Wbwk, Hmwk assignments, and contents of the textbook.\\
Let $(G, *)$ be a group with identity $e \in G$.
\begin{define}{Subgroup}[def:subgroup][red]
  A nonempty subset $H$ of $G$ is called a subgroup of $G$ if (and only if) ( $H, *$ ) is a group. (That is, $H$ itself is a group with respect to the product of $G$.)
\end{define}
\begin{define}{Subgroup Lemma}[lem:subgroup-lemma][red]
  (To be proved on ICA15.) A subset $H \subseteq G$ is a subgroup of ( $G, *$ ) if (and only if) 
  $H$ is nonempty, closed under the product of $G$, and contains inverses, i.e 
  \begin{enumerate}
    \item $H \neq \emptyset$,
    \item for all $a, b \in H$, $a * b \in H$, and
    \item for all $a \in H, a^{-1} \in H$.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{define}
\begin{define}{Order of an Element}[def:order-of-an-element][red]
  Let $G$ be a finite group. The \textbf{order} of an 
  element $a \in G$, denoted $o(a)$, is the least positive 
  integer $m$ such that $a^m=e$.
\end{define}
\begin{define}{Cyclic Subgroup}[def:cyclic-subgroup][red]
  The cyclic subgroup generated by $a \in G$ is the 
  set, $\left(a\right)=\left\{a^i \mid i \in \setZ\right\}$.
\end{define}
\begin{define}{Abelian Group}[def:abelian-group][red]
  A group $(G, *)$ is called an \textbf{abelian group} (or \textbf{commutative group}) if for all $a, b \in G$, 
  \[
    a * b = b * a.
  \]
\end{define}
